Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I came in because... I just can't shake this thought. You know, I've been an electrician for a long time. Over twenty years. I actually went to college for a couple semesters first, thinking I'd do business or something, but sitting in a classroom just drove me crazy. So I got into the trades. Best decision I made, usually. The union has good benefits, and you get to actually work with your hands. 

I was actually just telling this guy at the hardware store yesterday—he was trying to buy the completely wrong gauge wire for a DIY home project, and I had to step in and stop him before he burned his whole house down. People don't respect electricity, you know? They just think it's magic behind the drywall and they don't realize how fast things can go wrong if you don't know what you're doing. 

But... yeah, anyway, the reason I'm here is... I just can't stop thinking that if all my years doing this, all that experience as an electrician, couldn't keep my own brother safe... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who lives under my roof. I just feel like I can't be trusted to keep anyone safe anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think I've ever felt like this before. I mean, sure, everyone gets down sometimes, right? Like when the economy took a hit back in, what was it, '08? Work dried up real fast. I remember sitting at the union hall for weeks just waiting for a call. That was stressful. I actually ended up fixing up this old '69 Mustang during that time. Beautiful car, cherry red. Found it rusting in a guy's barn out in the county. Took me about six months to rebuild the engine, completely rewired the whole thing myself, obviously. Had to track down original parts from this guy in Ohio who ran a salvage yard just for classic Fords. He was a real character, had this massive dog that would just sit on the hoods of the cars... but no, I never felt hopeless like I do now. Or like I was a danger. This is different.

nephew shut the garage door and suddenly I'm right back smelling smoke and seeing my brother, like the job site drops out from under me.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: people down fast if they start talking about car projects or the fire.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, The union has good benefits, and you get to actually work with your hands. \n\nI was actually just telling this guy at the hardware store yesterday—he was trying to buy the completely wrong gauge wire for a DIY home project, and I had to step in and stop him before he burned his whole house down. My wife got him a new console for his birthday because all his stuff was ruined, of course. She's been great through all this. I haven't really noticed, honestly. I guess I haven't been out in the garage as much, but... well, the weather's been turning, too. It gets real drafty out there. I actually spent a whole weekend last fall trying to insulate the garage doors myself. Bought those foam panels from the big box store, you know? Cut them all to size, taped them in. It helped a little, but the real problem is the seal at the bottom. It's totally shot. Every time it rains heavy, water pools right near my workbench. I keep meaning to replace the weather stripping, but you have to order the exact right profile for those older doors, and the company that made them went out of business back in the nineties. So you gotta hunt around online. My buddy Dave—he's a plumber, we've done some side jobs together—he told me about this website that sells universal seals, but I just haven't gotten around to looking it up. Dave's a good guy, actually just had his first grandkid last month. A little girl. He was showing me pictures on his phone... but yeah, I don't know if I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't really been doing them.

Focus? Uh, I don't know. I mean, my job takes a lot of focus, obviously. Being an electrician, you can't really afford to lose focus or you'll get yourself fried. We were working on this commercial build downtown a couple weeks ago, one of those new mixed-use buildings they're throwing up everywhere. Retail on the bottom, overpriced apartments on top. The general contractor was this guy, total micromanager. He kept changing the specs on the lighting fixtures for the lobby. First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.

I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. Or doing much of anything, really. I used to go fishing down at the reservoir on Sundays, but they've been doing all this construction down there on the dam. Trying to reinforce it or something. He makes his own lures, hand-paints them and everything. Really intricate work. He was telling me last time I was in there that the state is trying to buy him out, use eminent domain to widen the highway. It's a shame, you know? Small businesses like that just getting pushed out. My wife's brother tried to start a landscaping business a few years back, bought two zero-turn mowers and a trailer, but he couldn't compete with the big commercial outfits. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't know if it's that I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't had the energy to bother with them.

I mean, yeah, I guess. That's actually why I came in. I just... I can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. It's just... The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I had a buddy, Dave, he was using a flex bit on a job in the South End, and he drilled right through the main water line. Flooded the entire first floor of this doctor's house. The insurance claim was a nightmare. They had to rip up these original hardwood floors, Brazilian cherry or something. Dave ended up having to close his business and go work for the union because his premiums went through the roof. The union's okay, I guess, but the dues are crazy and the politics... don't even get me started on the politics. The shop steward at my last company was this guy who spent more time in the trailer drinking coffee than actually turning a screwdriver. But yeah... I just feel like it's my fault. I should have known better.

Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just can't shake this thought. You know, I've been an electrician for a long time. Over twenty years. I actually went to college for a couple semesters first, thinking I'd do business or something, but sitting in a classroom just drove me crazy. So I got into the trades. Best decision I made, usually. The union has good benefits, and you get to actually work with your hands. \n\nI was actually just telling this guy at the hardware store yesterday—he was trying to buy the completely wrong gauge wire for a DIY home project, and I had to step in and stop him before he burned his whole house down. People don't respect electricity, you know? I just can't stop thinking that if all my years doing this, all that experience as an electrician, couldn't keep my own brother safe... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who lives under my roof. I just feel like I can't be trusted to keep anyone safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or had a time when you felt down, hopeless, or had thoughts that you were a danger to others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like this before. I mean, sure, everyone gets down sometimes, right? Like when the economy took a hit back in, what was it, '08? Work dried up real fast. I remember sitting at the union hall for weeks just waiting for a call. That was stressful. I actually ended up fixing up this old '69 Mustang during that time. Beautiful car, cherry red. Found it rusting in a guy's barn out in the county. Took me about six months to rebuild the engine, completely rewired the whole thing myself, obviously. Had to track down original parts from this guy in Ohio who ran a salvage yard just for classic Fords. He was a real character, had this massive dog that would just sit on the hoods of the cars... but no, I never felt hopeless like I do now. Or like I was a danger. This is different.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have these thoughts been present most days, for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about four months now. Yeah, right around four months, pretty much every day, all day. It started right after the garage fire... and then my nephew had to come live with us. Which, you know, we were glad to take him in, obviously. He's a good kid. A little quiet, but good. He's really into those video games where you build stuff—blocks or whatever. My wife got him a new console for his birthday because all his stuff was ruined, of course. She's been great through all this. I guess I haven't been out in the garage as much, but... well, the weather's been turning, too. It gets real drafty out there. I actually spent a whole weekend last fall trying to insulate the garage doors myself. Bought those foam panels from the big box store, you know? Cut them all to size, taped them in. It helped a little, but the real problem is the seal at the bottom. It's totally shot. Every time it rains heavy, water pools right near my workbench. I keep meaning to replace the weather stripping, but you have to order the exact right profile for those older doors, and the company that made them went out of business back in the nineties. So you gotta hunt around online. My buddy Dave—he's a plumber, we've done some side jobs together—he told me about this website that sells universal seals, but I just haven't gotten around to looking it up. Dave's a good guy, actually just had his first grandkid last month. A little girl. He was showing me pictures on his phone... but yeah, I don't know if I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't really been doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly since this started?\n\nPatient: Focus? Uh, I don't know. I mean, my job takes a lot of focus, obviously. Being an electrician, you can't really afford to lose focus or you'll get yourself fried. We were working on this commercial build downtown a couple weeks ago, one of those new mixed-use buildings they're throwing up everywhere. Retail on the bottom, overpriced apartments on top. The general contractor was this guy, total micromanager. He kept changing the specs on the lighting fixtures for the lobby. First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. The ones where they buy a total dump and fix it up in like three weeks. It's totally unrealistic, the timelines they show. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. They brought in these massive cranes, and the noise is just unbelievable. You can't even hear yourself think, let alone relax. Plus, they blocked off the best access road, the one that goes right by the old bait shop. The guy who runs that shop, Ernie, he's been there since I was a kid. He makes his own lures, hand-paints them and everything. Really intricate work. He was telling me last time I was in there that the state is trying to buy him out, use eminent domain to widen the highway. It's a shame, you know? Small businesses like that just getting pushed out. My wife's brother tried to start a landscaping business a few years back, bought two zero-turn mowers and a trailer, but he couldn't compete with the big commercial outfits. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. That's actually why I came in. I just... The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But no, like I said, I haven't really been doing any of that stuff. The fishing thing, like I mentioned, the reservoir is just a mess right now with all that heavy machinery. And the garage... well, I've got my '68 Mustang in there. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. Which, you know, we were glad to take him in, obviously. He's a good kid. A little quiet, but good. He's really into those video games where you build stuff—blocks or whatever. My wife got him a new console for his birthday because all his stuff was ruined, of course. She's been great through all this. She works at the elementary school, front office, so she knows how to talk to kids who are going through a hard time. She actually organized a bake sale last year to raise money for the playground equipment. They got this huge new slide, one of those spiral ones, and the kids just go crazy for it. But yeah, ever since the fire and him moving into the spare room... I just wake up with it, and it sits on my chest all day while I'm at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or other hobbies?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed, honestly. I guess I haven't been out in the garage as much, but... well, the weather's been turning, too. It gets real drafty out there. I actually spent a whole weekend last fall trying to insulate the garage doors myself. Bought those foam panels from the big box store, you know? Cut them all to size, taped them in. It helped a little, but the real problem is the seal at the bottom. It's totally shot. Every time it rains heavy, water pools right near my workbench. I keep meaning to replace the weather stripping, but you have to order the exact right profile for those older doors, and the company that made them went out of business back in the nineties. So you gotta hunt around online. My buddy Dave—he's a plumber, we've done some side jobs together—he told me about this website that sells universal seals, but I just haven't gotten around to looking it up. Dave's a good guy, actually just had his first grandkid last month. A little girl. He was showing me pictures on his phone... but yeah, I don't know if I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't really been doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly since this started?\n\nPatient: Focus? Uh, I don't know. I mean, my job takes a lot of focus, obviously. Being an electrician, you can't really afford to lose focus or you'll get yourself fried. We were working on this commercial build downtown a couple weeks ago, one of those new mixed-use buildings they're throwing up everywhere. Retail on the bottom, overpriced apartments on top. The general contractor was this guy, total micromanager. He kept changing the specs on the lighting fixtures for the lobby. First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. The ones where they buy a total dump and fix it up in like three weeks. It's totally unrealistic, the timelines they show. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. Or doing much of anything, really. I used to go fishing down at the reservoir on Sundays, but they've been doing all this construction down there on the dam. Trying to reinforce it or something. They brought in these massive cranes, and the noise is just unbelievable. You can't even hear yourself think, let alone relax. Plus, they blocked off the best access road, the one that goes right by the old bait shop. The guy who runs that shop, Ernie, he's been there since I was a kid. He makes his own lures, hand-paints them and everything. Really intricate work. He was telling me last time I was in there that the state is trying to buy him out, use eminent domain to widen the highway. It's a shame, you know? Small businesses like that just getting pushed out. My wife's brother tried to start a landscaping business a few years back, bought two zero-turn mowers and a trailer, but he couldn't compete with the big commercial outfits. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't know if it's that I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't had the energy to bother with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're a failure or blamed yourself for things more than usual since this started?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess. That's actually why I came in. I just... I can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. It's just... I should have checked his wiring. I told him when he bought that house that those old Victorian places are a nightmare. The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I had a buddy, Dave, he was using a flex bit on a job in the South End, and he drilled right through the main water line. Flooded the entire first floor of this doctor's house. The insurance claim was a nightmare. They had to rip up these original hardwood floors, Brazilian cherry or something. Dave ended up having to close his business and go work for the union because his premiums went through the roof. The union's okay, I guess, but the dues are crazy and the politics... don't even get me started on the politics. The shop steward at my last company was this guy who spent more time in the trailer drinking coffee than actually turning a screwdriver. But yeah... I just feel like it's my fault. I should have known better.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don’t interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But no, like I said, I haven't really been doing any of that stuff. The fishing thing, like I mentioned, the reservoir is just a mess right now with all that heavy machinery. And the garage... well, I've got my '68 Mustang in there. I've been restoring it for, gosh, almost ten years now. It's a slow process when you're doing it right. I finally tracked down an original carburetor for it last month. Found this guy online, lives all the way out in Idaho. He's got a whole barn full of classic Ford parts. It's crazy what people hold onto. He was telling me he bought out an old dealership that went under in the seventies. Just packed everything into crates. You have to be careful with those old carbs, though. The ethanol in modern gas just eats right through the rubber gaskets and seals. It's a real headache. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. But anyway, no, I haven't even taken the carburetor out of the box since it arrived. Just haven't cared enough to go out there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I finally tracked down an original carburetor for it last month. Found this guy online, lives all the way out in Idaho. He's got a whole barn full of classic Ford parts. It's crazy what people hold onto. He was telling me he bought out an old dealership that went under in the seventies. Just packed everything into crates. You have to be careful with those old carbs, though. The ethanol in modern gas just eats right through the rubber gaskets and seals. It's a real headache. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. But anyway, no, I haven't even taken the carburetor out of the box since it arrived. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like we're going in circles here, Doc. You keep asking me the same thing. But yeah, like I said, I just don't get any kick out of it anymore. It's like... I don't know, the spark is gone. Speaking of sparks, you wouldn't believe the shoddy electrical work I saw at the grocery store the other day. They were putting in these new self-checkout lanes, right? And the contractor they hired, I don't even know if they were licensed. They had Romex wire just exposed, zip-tied to the metal shelving. That's a huge code violation. If a shopping cart hits that and nicks the insulation, you've got a live frame. Somebody touches it, bam, they're getting a shock. I tried to point it out to the manager, this kid who looked like he was barely out of high school, and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. It's frustrating. People just don't respect the trade anymore. Everybody wants things done cheap and fast. It's like my neighbor, he tried to wire his own hot tub. Watched a couple of YouTube videos and thought he was an expert. Ended up tripping his main breaker every time he turned the jets on. I had to go over there and redo the whole subpanel. But anyway, yeah, I just... I haven't touched the car. Or my fishing gear. I just sit around, mostly.

Best decision I made, usually. It helped a little, but the real problem is the seal at the bottom. It's totally shot. Every time it rains heavy, water pools right near my workbench. I keep meaning to replace the weather stripping, but you have to order the exact right profile for those older doors, and the company that made them went out of business back in the nineties. So you gotta hunt around online. My buddy Dave—he's a plumber, we've done some side jobs together—he told me about this website that sells universal seals, but I just haven't gotten around to looking it up. Dave's a good guy, actually just had his first grandkid last month. A little girl. He was showing me pictures on his phone... but yeah, I don't know if I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't really been doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly since this started?\n\nPatient: Focus? The general contractor was this guy, total micromanager. He kept changing the specs on the lighting fixtures for the lobby. First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. I don't think so. I mean, not before all this happened with my brother. I've always been a pretty active guy, you know? Always had a project going. If it wasn't the Mustang, it was helping someone out with their house. Like my buddy Mark, he bought this total fixer-upper out in Worcester a few years back. The place was a disaster. We spent three weekends just tearing out old knob-and-tube wiring. You ever see that stuff? It's fascinating, really, the way they used to do it back in the twenties. They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. It was actually a pretty safe system if you didn't mess with it, because the wires were kept separate so they couldn't short out against each other. But the problem is the insulation, it's this old cloth and rubber stuff, and after eighty years it just crumbles to dust if you even look at it wrong. Plus, people would splice into it over the years, add new outlets, and they'd just twist the wires together and wrap them in friction tape. Total fire hazard. We ended up having to pull a whole new 200-amp service for his place. But yeah, to answer your question, no. I've never felt like this before. I've always loved keeping busy.

Best decision I made, usually. The union has good benefits, and you get to actually work with your hands. \n\nI was actually just telling this guy at the hardware store yesterday—he was trying to buy the completely wrong gauge wire for a DIY home project, and I had to step in and stop him before he burned his whole house down. People don't respect electricity, you know? I just feel like I can't be trusted to keep anyone safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or had a time when you felt down, hopeless, or had thoughts that you were a danger to others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like this before. I mean, sure, everyone gets down sometimes, right? Like when the economy took a hit back in, what was it, '08? Work dried up real fast. I remember sitting at the union hall for weeks just waiting for a call. That was stressful. I actually ended up fixing up this old '69 Mustang during that time. Beautiful car, cherry red. Found it rusting in a guy's barn out in the county. Took me about six months to rebuild the engine, completely rewired the whole thing myself, obviously. Had to track down original parts from this guy in Ohio who ran a salvage yard just for classic Fords. He was a real character, had this massive dog that would just sit on the hoods of the cars... but no, I never felt hopeless like I do now. Or like I was a danger. This is different.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have these thoughts been present most days, for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about four months now. Yeah, right around four months, pretty much every day, all day. It started right after the garage fire... and then my nephew had to come live with us. Which, you know, we were glad to take him in, obviously. He's a good kid. A little quiet, but good. He's really into those video games where you build stuff—blocks or whatever. My wife got him a new console for his birthday because all his stuff was ruined, of course. She's been great through all this. She works at the elementary school, front office, so she knows how to talk to kids who are going through a hard time. She actually organized a bake sale last year to raise money for the playground equipment. They got this huge new slide, one of those spiral ones, and the kids just go crazy for it. But yeah, ever since the fire and him moving into the spare room... I just wake up with it, and it sits on my chest all day while I'm at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or other hobbies?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed, honestly. I guess I haven't been out in the garage as much, but... well, the weather's been turning, too. It gets real drafty out there. I actually spent a whole weekend last fall trying to insulate the garage doors myself. Bought those foam panels from the big box store, you know? Cut them all to size, taped them in. It helped a little, but the real problem is the seal at the bottom. It's totally shot. Every time it rains heavy, water pools right near my workbench. I keep meaning to replace the weather stripping, but you have to order the exact right profile for those older doors, and the company that made them went out of business back in the nineties. So you gotta hunt around online. My buddy Dave—he's a plumber, we've done some side jobs together—he told me about this website that sells universal seals, but I just haven't gotten around to looking it up. Dave's a good guy, actually just had his first grandkid last month. A little girl. He was showing me pictures on his phone... but yeah, I don't know if I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't really been doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly since this started?\n\nPatient: Focus? Uh, I don't know. I mean, my job takes a lot of focus, obviously. Being an electrician, you can't really afford to lose focus or you'll get yourself fried. We were working on this commercial build downtown a couple weeks ago, one of those new mixed-use buildings they're throwing up everywhere. Retail on the bottom, overpriced apartments on top. The general contractor was this guy, total micromanager. He kept changing the specs on the lighting fixtures for the lobby. First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. The ones where they buy a total dump and fix it up in like three weeks. It's totally unrealistic, the timelines they show. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. Or doing much of anything, really. I used to go fishing down at the reservoir on Sundays, but they've been doing all this construction down there on the dam. Trying to reinforce it or something. They brought in these massive cranes, and the noise is just unbelievable. You can't even hear yourself think, let alone relax. Plus, they blocked off the best access road, the one that goes right by the old bait shop. The guy who runs that shop, Ernie, he's been there since I was a kid. He makes his own lures, hand-paints them and everything. Really intricate work. He was telling me last time I was in there that the state is trying to buy him out, use eminent domain to widen the highway. It's a shame, you know? Small businesses like that just getting pushed out. My wife's brother tried to start a landscaping business a few years back, bought two zero-turn mowers and a trailer, but he couldn't compete with the big commercial outfits. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't know if it's that I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't had the energy to bother with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're a failure or blamed yourself for things more than usual since this started?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess. That's actually why I came in. I just... I can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. It's just... I should have checked his wiring. I told him when he bought that house that those old Victorian places are a nightmare. The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I had a buddy, Dave, he was using a flex bit on a job in the South End, and he drilled right through the main water line. Flooded the entire first floor of this doctor's house. The insurance claim was a nightmare. They had to rip up these original hardwood floors, Brazilian cherry or something. Dave ended up having to close his business and go work for the union because his premiums went through the roof. The union's okay, I guess, but the dues are crazy and the politics... don't even get me started on the politics. The shop steward at my last company was this guy who spent more time in the trailer drinking coffee than actually turning a screwdriver. But yeah... I just feel like it's my fault. I should have known better.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don’t interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But no, like I said, I haven't really been doing any of that stuff. The fishing thing, like I mentioned, the reservoir is just a mess right now with all that heavy machinery. And the garage... well, I've got my '68 Mustang in there. I've been restoring it for, gosh, almost ten years now. It's a slow process when you're doing it right. I finally tracked down an original carburetor for it last month. Found this guy online, lives all the way out in Idaho. He's got a whole barn full of classic Ford parts. It's crazy what people hold onto. He was telling me he bought out an old dealership that went under in the seventies. Just packed everything into crates. You have to be careful with those old carbs, though. The ethanol in modern gas just eats right through the rubber gaskets and seals. It's a real headache. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. But anyway, no, I haven't even taken the carburetor out of the box since it arrived. Just haven't cared enough to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or enjoyment from things you used to really like doing, like working on your car or fishing?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here, Doc. You keep asking me the same thing. But yeah, like I said, I just don't get any kick out of it anymore. It's like... I don't know, the spark is gone. Speaking of sparks, you wouldn't believe the shoddy electrical work I saw at the grocery store the other day. They were putting in these new self-checkout lanes, right? And the contractor they hired, I don't even know if they were licensed. They had Romex wire just exposed, zip-tied to the metal shelving. That's a huge code violation. If a shopping cart hits that and nicks the insulation, you've got a live frame. Somebody touches it, bam, they're getting a shock. I tried to point it out to the manager, this kid who looked like he was barely out of high school, and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. It's frustrating. People just don't respect the trade anymore. Everybody wants things done cheap and fast. It's like my neighbor, he tried to wire his own hot tub. Watched a couple of YouTube videos and thought he was an expert. Ended up tripping his main breaker every time he turned the jets on. I had to go over there and redo the whole subpanel. But anyway, yeah, I just... I haven't touched the car. Or my fishing gear. I just sit around, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past when you stopped enjoying things you used to really like doing, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, not before all this happened with my brother. I've always been a pretty active guy, you know? Always had a project going. If it wasn't the Mustang, it was helping someone out with their house. Like my buddy Mark, he bought this total fixer-upper out in Worcester a few years back. The place was a disaster. We spent three weekends just tearing out old knob-and-tube wiring. You ever see that stuff? It's fascinating, really, the way they used to do it back in the twenties. They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. It was actually a pretty safe system if you didn't mess with it, because the wires were kept separate so they couldn't short out against each other. But the problem is the insulation, it's this old cloth and rubber stuff, and after eighty years it just crumbles to dust if you even look at it wrong. Plus, people would splice into it over the years, add new outlets, and they'd just twist the wires together and wrap them in friction tape. Total fire hazard. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Energy? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling sluggish or anything like that. I'm just... sitting around, mostly. But speaking of energy, you know what's getting really crazy lately? The new solar setups people are putting in. I mean, the battery banks alone... I had a guy call me a few months back wanting to go completely off-grid. He bought this massive lithium-iron-phosphate battery system. Those things are incredibly heavy, let me tell you, and the wiring requirements are pretty strict. 

And the inverters they use now, the pure sine wave ones, they're so much better than the modified sine wave junk we used to install ten years ago. If you run sensitive electronics on a modified sine wave, it just wreaks havoc on the power supplies. You'll hear this loud humming noise coming from the transformers. A lot of guys don't realize that and they end up frying their flat screens or their computers because they tried to save a few bucks on the inverter. It's all about matching the load to the capacity and getting clean power. People just buy stuff off Amazon and think they can wire it up themselves without understanding voltage drop. But anyway... what were you asking? Oh, if I'm tired. No, my energy is fine, I guess. I just don't feel like doing much with it.

First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. The ones where they buy a total dump and fix it up in like three weeks. It's totally unrealistic, the timelines they show. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't know if it's that I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't had the energy to bother with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're a failure or blamed yourself for things more than usual since this started?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess. The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I had a buddy, Dave, he was using a flex bit on a job in the South End, and he drilled right through the main water line. Flooded the entire first floor of this doctor's house. The insurance claim was a nightmare. They had to rip up these original hardwood floors, Brazilian cherry or something. Dave ended up having to close his business and go work for the union because his premiums went through the roof. The union's okay, I guess, but the dues are crazy and the politics... don't even get me started on the politics. The shop steward at my last company was this guy who spent more time in the trailer drinking coffee than actually turning a screwdriver. But yeah... I just feel like it's my fault. I should have known better.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don’t interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about this, didn't we? They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. The ones where they buy a total dump and fix it up in like three weeks. It's totally unrealistic, the timelines they show. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. Or doing much of anything, really. I used to go fishing down at the reservoir on Sundays, but they've been doing all this construction down there on the dam. Trying to reinforce it or something. They brought in these massive cranes, and the noise is just unbelievable. You can't even hear yourself think, let alone relax. Plus, they blocked off the best access road, the one that goes right by the old bait shop. The guy who runs that shop, Ernie, he's been there since I was a kid. He makes his own lures, hand-paints them and everything. Really intricate work. He was telling me last time I was in there that the state is trying to buy him out, use eminent domain to widen the highway. It's a shame, you know? Small businesses like that just getting pushed out. My wife's brother tried to start a landscaping business a few years back, bought two zero-turn mowers and a trailer, but he couldn't compete with the big commercial outfits. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't know if it's that I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't had the energy to bother with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're a failure or blamed yourself for things more than usual since this started?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess. That's actually why I came in. I just... I can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. It's just... I should have checked his wiring. I told him when he bought that house that those old Victorian places are a nightmare. The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I had a buddy, Dave, he was using a flex bit on a job in the South End, and he drilled right through the main water line. Flooded the entire first floor of this doctor's house. The insurance claim was a nightmare. They had to rip up these original hardwood floors, Brazilian cherry or something. Dave ended up having to close his business and go work for the union because his premiums went through the roof. The union's okay, I guess, but the dues are crazy and the politics... don't even get me started on the politics. The shop steward at my last company was this guy who spent more time in the trailer drinking coffee than actually turning a screwdriver. But yeah... I just feel like it's my fault. I should have known better.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don’t interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But no, like I said, I haven't really been doing any of that stuff. The fishing thing, like I mentioned, the reservoir is just a mess right now with all that heavy machinery. And the garage... well, I've got my '68 Mustang in there. I've been restoring it for, gosh, almost ten years now. It's a slow process when you're doing it right. I finally tracked down an original carburetor for it last month. Found this guy online, lives all the way out in Idaho. He's got a whole barn full of classic Ford parts. It's crazy what people hold onto. He was telling me he bought out an old dealership that went under in the seventies. Just packed everything into crates. You have to be careful with those old carbs, though. The ethanol in modern gas just eats right through the rubber gaskets and seals. It's a real headache. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. But anyway, no, I haven't even taken the carburetor out of the box since it arrived. Just haven't cared enough to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or enjoyment from things you used to really like doing, like working on your car or fishing?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here, Doc. You keep asking me the same thing. But yeah, like I said, I just don't get any kick out of it anymore. It's like... I don't know, the spark is gone. Speaking of sparks, you wouldn't believe the shoddy electrical work I saw at the grocery store the other day. They were putting in these new self-checkout lanes, right? And the contractor they hired, I don't even know if they were licensed. They had Romex wire just exposed, zip-tied to the metal shelving. That's a huge code violation. If a shopping cart hits that and nicks the insulation, you've got a live frame. Somebody touches it, bam, they're getting a shock. I tried to point it out to the manager, this kid who looked like he was barely out of high school, and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. It's frustrating. People just don't respect the trade anymore. Everybody wants things done cheap and fast. It's like my neighbor, he tried to wire his own hot tub. Watched a couple of YouTube videos and thought he was an expert. Ended up tripping his main breaker every time he turned the jets on. I had to go over there and redo the whole subpanel. But anyway, yeah, I just... I haven't touched the car. Or my fishing gear. I just sit around, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past when you stopped enjoying things you used to really like doing, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, not before all this happened with my brother. I've always been a pretty active guy, you know? Always had a project going. If it wasn't the Mustang, it was helping someone out with their house. Like my buddy Mark, he bought this total fixer-upper out in Worcester a few years back. The place was a disaster. We spent three weekends just tearing out old knob-and-tube wiring. You ever see that stuff? It's fascinating, really, the way they used to do it back in the twenties. They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. It was actually a pretty safe system if you didn't mess with it, because the wires were kept separate so they couldn't short out against each other. But the problem is the insulation, it's this old cloth and rubber stuff, and after eighty years it just crumbles to dust if you even look at it wrong. Plus, people would splice into it over the years, add new outlets, and they'd just twist the wires together and wrap them in friction tape. Total fire hazard. We ended up having to pull a whole new 200-amp service for his place. But yeah, to answer your question, no. I've never felt like this before. I've always loved keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired or sluggish than usual?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling sluggish or anything like that. I'm just... sitting around, mostly. But speaking of energy, you know what's getting really crazy lately? The new solar setups people are putting in. I mean, the battery banks alone... I had a guy call me a few months back wanting to go completely off-grid. He bought this massive lithium-iron-phosphate battery system. Those things are incredibly heavy, let me tell you, and the wiring requirements are pretty strict. \n\nAnd the inverters they use now, the pure sine wave ones, they're so much better than the modified sine wave junk we used to install ten years ago. If you run sensitive electronics on a modified sine wave, it just wreaks havoc on the power supplies. You'll hear this loud humming noise coming from the transformers. A lot of guys don't realize that and they end up frying their flat screens or their computers because they tried to save a few bucks on the inverter. It's all about matching the load to the capacity and getting clean power. People just buy stuff off Amazon and think they can wire it up themselves without understanding voltage drop. But anyway... what were you asking? Oh, if I'm tired. No, my energy is fine, I guess. I just don't feel like doing much with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doc, we literally just talked about this. I already told you, the spark is gone. I haven't touched the Mustang, haven't looked at my fishing rods... it's just sitting there. But you know what doesn't just sit there? Rust. I went out to the garage the other day just to grab a screwdriver, and I noticed the quarter panels on the Mustang are starting to bubble a little bit near the wheel wells. That's the problem with those old Fords, the drainage in the cowl area was terrible. Leaves and pine needles get down in there, trap the moisture, and before you know it, you've got rot all the way through the firewall. 

I was actually thinking about cutting out the bad metal and welding in some patch panels. You've got to use a MIG welder for that kind of sheet metal work, TIG is too slow and flux core just burns right through it. You get a nice 75/25 argon-CO2 mix, dial in the wire speed, and just do these little spot welds so you don't warp the panel from the heat. It's a whole art form, really. My buddy Dave, he does auto body full time, he was showing me how to use a shrinking disc to get the metal perfectly flat before you even touch the body filler. Most guys just slather Bondo over everything and call it a day, but that stuff cracks after a couple of years. You want to use lead if you're doing it old school, but nobody does lead wiping anymore because of the toxicity. Anyway... yeah. Like I said before, I just don't have the interest in it right now. It's just sitting there rusting.

Best decision I made, usually. The union has good benefits, and you get to actually work with your hands. \n\nI was actually just telling this guy at the hardware store yesterday—he was trying to buy the completely wrong gauge wire for a DIY home project, and I had to step in and stop him before he burned his whole house down. I just feel like I can't be trusted to keep anyone safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, or had a time when you felt down, hopeless, or had thoughts that you were a danger to others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever felt like this before. I remember sitting at the union hall for weeks just waiting for a call. That was stressful. I actually ended up fixing up this old '69 Mustang during that time. Beautiful car, cherry red. Had to track down original parts from this guy in Ohio who ran a salvage yard just for classic Fords. He was a real character, had this massive dog that would just sit on the hoods of the cars... but no, I never felt hopeless like I do now. Or like I was a danger. This is different.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have these thoughts been present most days, for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about four months now. Yeah, right around four months, pretty much every day, all day. It started right after the garage fire... and then my nephew had to come live with us. Which, you know, we were glad to take him in, obviously. He's a good kid. A little quiet, but good. He's really into those video games where you build stuff—blocks or whatever. My wife got him a new console for his birthday because all his stuff was ruined, of course. She's been great through all this. She works at the elementary school, front office, so she knows how to talk to kids who are going through a hard time. She actually organized a bake sale last year to raise money for the playground equipment. They got this huge new slide, one of those spiral ones, and the kids just go crazy for it. But yeah, ever since the fire and him moving into the spare room... I just wake up with it, and it sits on my chest all day while I'm at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or other hobbies?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed, honestly. I guess I haven't been out in the garage as much, but... well, the weather's been turning, too. It gets real drafty out there. I actually spent a whole weekend last fall trying to insulate the garage doors myself. Bought those foam panels from the big box store, you know? Cut them all to size, taped them in. It helped a little, but the real problem is the seal at the bottom. It's totally shot. Every time it rains heavy, water pools right near my workbench. I keep meaning to replace the weather stripping, but you have to order the exact right profile for those older doors, and the company that made them went out of business back in the nineties. So you gotta hunt around online. My buddy Dave—he's a plumber, we've done some side jobs together—he told me about this website that sells universal seals, but I just haven't gotten around to looking it up. Dave's a good guy, actually just had his first grandkid last month. A little girl. He was showing me pictures on his phone... but yeah, I don't know if I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't really been doing them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to focus, make decisions, or think clearly since this started?\n\nPatient: Focus? Uh, I don't know. I mean, my job takes a lot of focus, obviously. Being an electrician, you can't really afford to lose focus or you'll get yourself fried. We were working on this commercial build downtown a couple weeks ago, one of those new mixed-use buildings they're throwing up everywhere. Retail on the bottom, overpriced apartments on top. The general contractor was this guy, total micromanager. He kept changing the specs on the lighting fixtures for the lobby. First he wanted these recessed LEDs, then he decided he wanted these big, industrial-looking pendant lights that weighed a ton. We had to completely reinforce the ceiling boxes. Took three of us just to hoist one of those things up there. And the scaffolding they rented for us was super sketchy, missing a cross brace on the second tier. I told the site super about it, but you know how it goes, they're always behind schedule and over budget. They ended up pouring the concrete for the sidewalk out front while we were still trying to run the exterior conduit. What a mess. But yeah, I haven't really noticed any problems making decisions or whatever. I just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life since this started?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'd never do that to my wife, especially not now with my nephew living with us. He's been through enough, you know? The fire marshal said it was a miracle they got him out when they did. The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. The ones where they buy a total dump and fix it up in like three weeks. It's totally unrealistic, the timelines they show. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. Or doing much of anything, really. I used to go fishing down at the reservoir on Sundays, but they've been doing all this construction down there on the dam. Trying to reinforce it or something. They brought in these massive cranes, and the noise is just unbelievable. You can't even hear yourself think, let alone relax. Plus, they blocked off the best access road, the one that goes right by the old bait shop. The guy who runs that shop, Ernie, he's been there since I was a kid. He makes his own lures, hand-paints them and everything. Really intricate work. He was telling me last time I was in there that the state is trying to buy him out, use eminent domain to widen the highway. It's a shame, you know? Small businesses like that just getting pushed out. My wife's brother tried to start a landscaping business a few years back, bought two zero-turn mowers and a trailer, but he couldn't compete with the big commercial outfits. He ended up selling the equipment at a huge loss. But yeah, to answer your question, I don't know if it's that I'm not enjoying things, I just haven't had the energy to bother with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're a failure or blamed yourself for things more than usual since this started?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess. That's actually why I came in. I just... I can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. It's just... I should have checked his wiring. I told him when he bought that house that those old Victorian places are a nightmare. The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. I had a buddy, Dave, he was using a flex bit on a job in the South End, and he drilled right through the main water line. Flooded the entire first floor of this doctor's house. The insurance claim was a nightmare. They had to rip up these original hardwood floors, Brazilian cherry or something. Dave ended up having to close his business and go work for the union because his premiums went through the roof. The union's okay, I guess, but the dues are crazy and the politics... don't even get me started on the politics. The shop steward at my last company was this guy who spent more time in the trailer drinking coffee than actually turning a screwdriver. But yeah... I just feel like it's my fault. I should have known better.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don’t interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But no, like I said, I haven't really been doing any of that stuff. The fishing thing, like I mentioned, the reservoir is just a mess right now with all that heavy machinery. And the garage... well, I've got my '68 Mustang in there. I've been restoring it for, gosh, almost ten years now. It's a slow process when you're doing it right. I finally tracked down an original carburetor for it last month. Found this guy online, lives all the way out in Idaho. He's got a whole barn full of classic Ford parts. It's crazy what people hold onto. He was telling me he bought out an old dealership that went under in the seventies. Just packed everything into crates. You have to be careful with those old carbs, though. The ethanol in modern gas just eats right through the rubber gaskets and seals. It's a real headache. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. But anyway, no, I haven't even taken the carburetor out of the box since it arrived. Just haven't cared enough to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or enjoyment from things you used to really like doing, like working on your car or fishing?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here, Doc. You keep asking me the same thing. But yeah, like I said, I just don't get any kick out of it anymore. It's like... I don't know, the spark is gone. Speaking of sparks, you wouldn't believe the shoddy electrical work I saw at the grocery store the other day. They were putting in these new self-checkout lanes, right? And the contractor they hired, I don't even know if they were licensed. They had Romex wire just exposed, zip-tied to the metal shelving. That's a huge code violation. If a shopping cart hits that and nicks the insulation, you've got a live frame. Somebody touches it, bam, they're getting a shock. I tried to point it out to the manager, this kid who looked like he was barely out of high school, and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. It's frustrating. People just don't respect the trade anymore. Everybody wants things done cheap and fast. It's like my neighbor, he tried to wire his own hot tub. Watched a couple of YouTube videos and thought he was an expert. Ended up tripping his main breaker every time he turned the jets on. I had to go over there and redo the whole subpanel. But anyway, yeah, I just... I haven't touched the car. Or my fishing gear. I just sit around, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past when you stopped enjoying things you used to really like doing, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, not before all this happened with my brother. I've always been a pretty active guy, you know? Always had a project going. If it wasn't the Mustang, it was helping someone out with their house. Like my buddy Mark, he bought this total fixer-upper out in Worcester a few years back. The place was a disaster. We spent three weekends just tearing out old knob-and-tube wiring. You ever see that stuff? It's fascinating, really, the way they used to do it back in the twenties. They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. It was actually a pretty safe system if you didn't mess with it, because the wires were kept separate so they couldn't short out against each other. But the problem is the insulation, it's this old cloth and rubber stuff, and after eighty years it just crumbles to dust if you even look at it wrong. Plus, people would splice into it over the years, add new outlets, and they'd just twist the wires together and wrap them in friction tape. Total fire hazard. We ended up having to pull a whole new 200-amp service for his place. But yeah, to answer your question, no. I've never felt like this before. I've always loved keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired or sluggish than usual?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling sluggish or anything like that. I'm just... sitting around, mostly. But speaking of energy, you know what's getting really crazy lately? The new solar setups people are putting in. I mean, the battery banks alone... I had a guy call me a few months back wanting to go completely off-grid. He bought this massive lithium-iron-phosphate battery system. Those things are incredibly heavy, let me tell you, and the wiring requirements are pretty strict. \n\nAnd the inverters they use now, the pure sine wave ones, they're so much better than the modified sine wave junk we used to install ten years ago. If you run sensitive electronics on a modified sine wave, it just wreaks havoc on the power supplies. You'll hear this loud humming noise coming from the transformers. A lot of guys don't realize that and they end up frying their flat screens or their computers because they tried to save a few bucks on the inverter. It's all about matching the load to the capacity and getting clean power. People just buy stuff off Amazon and think they can wire it up themselves without understanding voltage drop. But anyway... what were you asking? Oh, if I'm tired. No, my energy is fine, I guess. I just don't feel like doing much with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don't interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: Doc, we literally just talked about this. I already told you, the spark is gone. I haven't touched the Mustang, haven't looked at my fishing rods... it's just sitting there. But you know what doesn't just sit there? Rust. I went out to the garage the other day just to grab a screwdriver, and I noticed the quarter panels on the Mustang are starting to bubble a little bit near the wheel wells. That's the problem with those old Fords, the drainage in the cowl area was terrible. Leaves and pine needles get down in there, trap the moisture, and before you know it, you've got rot all the way through the firewall. \n\nI was actually thinking about cutting out the bad metal and welding in some patch panels. You've got to use a MIG welder for that kind of sheet metal work, TIG is too slow and flux core just burns right through it. You get a nice 75/25 argon-CO2 mix, dial in the wire speed, and just do these little spot welds so you don't warp the panel from the heat. It's a whole art form, really. My buddy Dave, he does auto body full time, he was showing me how to use a shrinking disc to get the metal perfectly flat before you even touch the body filler. Most guys just slather Bondo over everything and call it a day, but that stuff cracks after a couple of years. You want to use lead if you're doing it old school, but nobody does lead wiping anymore because of the toxicity. Anyway... yeah. Like I said before, I just don't have the interest in it right now. It's just sitting there rusting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
