Doc, are you reading off a script or something? I feel like I just answered this exact same thing. Yes, there's no pleasure in it, so it doesn't feel worth doing. It's like you're just going down a checklist. 

Which, man, speaking of checklists, you should see the punch lists the city electrical inspectors are handing out these days. It's like they don't even want you to finish a job. I had this one inspector down in Southie a while back, he made me rip out fifty feet of EMT conduit because it wasn't strapped exactly every three feet. The code says three feet, sure, but I had it at maybe three and a half because of where the studs were. He wouldn't budge. I had to go back and use these specialized masonry anchors into this hundred-year-old brick wall just to add more straps. 

You ever try to drill into old brick? It just turns to red powder. You need a heavy-duty hammer drill, like a Milwaukee SDS-plus, and even then, half the time the hole blows out and the anchor just spins. You end up having to pump it full of two-part epoxy just to get a simple tapcon screw to hold the strap. It completely ruins the profit margin on a job when you have to spend two whole days doing rework just to satisfy some guy with a clipboard who wants to feel important. 

But anyway... yeah. Same answer as two minutes ago. No pleasure, not worth the effort.

The whole back half of his house was just gone. It was an electrical fire, actually. Old wiring. Knobs and tubes, they call it. You still see it in those houses built before the fifties. People think they can just put a bigger breaker in the panel when it trips, but they don't realize the wire itself can't handle the load. It heats up, the insulation gets brittle and flakes off, and then boom, one spark and the dry wall catches. I tell people all the time, if you're buying an older home, you have to get a proper inspection. Not just some guy with a clipboard, an actual licensed electrician. But they never want to spend the money. They'd rather put it toward granite countertops or whatever the home improvement shows are pushing this week. My wife watches those shows religiously. But no, I haven't had any thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not enjoying things the way you used to, even things you really liked doing before?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think you asked me that already, but like I said, I just haven't been out in the garage much. The lath and plaster walls alone make it impossible to fish new wire without tearing everything apart. You have to use these special drill bits, flex bits, they're like four feet long and you have to blindly drill through the fire blocks inside the wall cavity. Half the time you end up hitting a plumbing pipe or getting the bit snagged on a nail. And the garage... well, I've got my '68 Mustang in there. I've been restoring it for, gosh, almost ten years now. It's a slow process when you're doing it right. I finally tracked down an original carburetor for it last month. Found this guy online, lives all the way out in Idaho. He's got a whole barn full of classic Ford parts. It's crazy what people hold onto. He was telling me he bought out an old dealership that went under in the seventies. Just packed everything into crates. You have to be careful with those old carbs, though. The ethanol in modern gas just eats right through the rubber gaskets and seals. It's a real headache. You basically have to rebuild them with special ethanol-resistant parts if you actually want to drive the car. There's a whole forum dedicated just to rebuilding Autolite carburetors. Some of those guys are absolute fanatics. They'll argue for days about the correct shade of zinc plating for the linkage rods. But anyway, no, I haven't even taken the carburetor out of the box since it arrived. Just haven't cared enough to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or enjoyment from things you used to really like doing, like working on your car or fishing?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here, Doc. You keep asking me the same thing. But yeah, like I said, I just don't get any kick out of it anymore. It's like... I don't know, the spark is gone. Speaking of sparks, you wouldn't believe the shoddy electrical work I saw at the grocery store the other day. They were putting in these new self-checkout lanes, right? And the contractor they hired, I don't even know if they were licensed. They had Romex wire just exposed, zip-tied to the metal shelving. That's a huge code violation. If a shopping cart hits that and nicks the insulation, you've got a live frame. Somebody touches it, bam, they're getting a shock. I tried to point it out to the manager, this kid who looked like he was barely out of high school, and he just stared at me like I was speaking Greek. It's frustrating. People just don't respect the trade anymore. Everybody wants things done cheap and fast. It's like my neighbor, he tried to wire his own hot tub. Watched a couple of YouTube videos and thought he was an expert. Ended up tripping his main breaker every time he turned the jets on. I had to go over there and redo the whole subpanel. But anyway, yeah, I just... I haven't touched the car. Or my fishing gear. I just sit around, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past when you stopped enjoying things you used to really like doing, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, not before all this happened with my brother. I've always been a pretty active guy, you know? Always had a project going. If it wasn't the Mustang, it was helping someone out with their house. Like my buddy Mark, he bought this total fixer-upper out in Worcester a few years back. The place was a disaster. We spent three weekends just tearing out old knob-and-tube wiring. You ever see that stuff? It's fascinating, really, the way they used to do it back in the twenties. They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. It was actually a pretty safe system if you didn't mess with it, because the wires were kept separate so they couldn't short out against each other. But the problem is the insulation, it's this old cloth and rubber stuff, and after eighty years it just crumbles to dust if you even look at it wrong. Plus, people would splice into it over the years, add new outlets, and they'd just twist the wires together and wrap them in friction tape. Total fire hazard. We ended up having to pull a whole new 200-amp service for his place. But yeah, to answer your question, no. I've never felt like this before. I've always loved keeping busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired or sluggish than usual?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling sluggish or anything like that. I'm just... sitting around, mostly. But speaking of energy, you know what's getting really crazy lately? The new solar setups people are putting in. I mean, the battery banks alone... I had a guy call me a few months back wanting to go completely off-grid. He bought this massive lithium-iron-phosphate battery system. Those things are incredibly heavy, let me tell you, and the wiring requirements are pretty strict. \n\nAnd the inverters they use now, the pure sine wave ones, they're so much better than the modified sine wave junk we used to install ten years ago. If you run sensitive electronics on a modified sine wave, it just wreaks havoc on the power supplies. You'll hear this loud humming noise coming from the transformers. A lot of guys don't realize that and they end up frying their flat screens or their computers because they tried to save a few bucks on the inverter. It's all about matching the load to the capacity and getting clean power. People just buy stuff off Amazon and think they can wire it up themselves without understanding voltage drop. But anyway... what were you asking? Oh, if I'm tired. No, my energy is fine, I guess. I just don't feel like doing much with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don't interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: Doc, we literally just talked about this. I already told you, the spark is gone. I haven't touched the Mustang, haven't looked at my fishing rods... it's just sitting there. But you know what doesn't just sit there? Rust. I went out to the garage the other day just to grab a screwdriver, and I noticed the quarter panels on the Mustang are starting to bubble a little bit near the wheel wells. That's the problem with those old Fords, the drainage in the cowl area was terrible. Leaves and pine needles get down in there, trap the moisture, and before you know it, you've got rot all the way through the firewall. \n\nI was actually thinking about cutting out the bad metal and welding in some patch panels. You've got to use a MIG welder for that kind of sheet metal work, TIG is too slow and flux core just burns right through it. You get a nice 75/25 argon-CO2 mix, dial in the wire speed, and just do these little spot welds so you don't warp the panel from the heat. It's a whole art form, really. My buddy Dave, he does auto body full time, he was showing me how to use a shrinking disc to get the metal perfectly flat before you even touch the body filler. Most guys just slather Bondo over everything and call it a day, but that stuff cracks after a couple of years. You want to use lead if you're doing it old school, but nobody does lead wiping anymore because of the toxicity. Anyway... yeah. Like I said before, I just don't have the interest in it right now. It's just sitting there rusting.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on your car or fishing, aren’t worth doing anymore because they don’t bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you reading off a script or something? I feel like I just answered this exact same thing. Yes, there's no pleasure in it, so it doesn't feel worth doing. It's like you're just going down a checklist. \n\nWhich, man, speaking of checklists, you should see the punch lists the city electrical inspectors are handing out these days. It's like they don't even want you to finish a job. I had this one inspector down in Southie a while back, he made me rip out fifty feet of EMT conduit because it wasn't strapped exactly every three feet. The code says three feet, sure, but I had it at maybe three and a half because of where the studs were. He wouldn't budge. I had to go back and use these specialized masonry anchors into this hundred-year-old brick wall just to add more straps. \n\nYou ever try to drill into old brick? It just turns to red powder. You need a heavy-duty hammer drill, like a Milwaukee SDS-plus, and even then, half the time the hole blows out and the anchor just spins. You end up having to pump it full of two-part epoxy just to get a simple tapcon screw to hold the strap. It completely ruins the profit margin on a job when you have to spend two whole days doing rework just to satisfy some guy with a clipboard who wants to feel important. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. Same answer as two minutes ago. No pleasure, not worth the effort.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Are you messing with me? Seriously, doc, is this a test? You literally just asked me that. Three times now. Yes. The answer is still yes. No pleasure, not worth doing. 

It's like dealing with my old apprentice, Tommy. Swear to God, I had to tell that kid the same thing five times a day. "Tommy, don't cross the neutral and the ground." "Tommy, make sure the breaker is actually off before you strip the wire." You know how many times I caught him working hot? It's a miracle he never got blown off a ladder. Working with 277-volt commercial lighting is no joke. It's not like the 120-volt stuff in your house where you get a little zap and pull your hand back. 277 will grab you, lock your muscles up, and throw you across the room. 

I used to have him wire up these massive high-bay LED fixtures in these old warehouses. The lifts we had to use, those scissor lifts, they get so wobbly when you're thirty feet up in the air. And Tommy would just be up there, swaying back and forth, not paying attention to where his fingers were. I finally had to let him go when he wired a three-way switch backward and almost fried a $5,000 lighting control panel. The client was furious. It's just... you can't teach common sense, you know? You can teach a guy how to bend conduit, how to calculate a load, but if he doesn't have the instinct to check his work... it's just a liability. 

But yeah. Like I said. I don't care about the car. I don't care about the fishing. I'm just tired of answering the same question.

We spent three weekends just tearing out old knob-and-tube wiring. You ever see that stuff? It's fascinating, really, the way they used to do it back in the twenties. They'd run these single insulated copper wires through porcelain tubes in the joists, and use these little porcelain knobs to keep the wires away from the wood. It was actually a pretty safe system if you didn't mess with it, because the wires were kept separate so they couldn't short out against each other. But the problem is the insulation, it's this old cloth and rubber stuff, and after eighty years it just crumbles to dust if you even look at it wrong. The new solar setups people are putting in. I mean, the battery banks alone... I had a guy call me a few months back wanting to go completely off-grid. He bought this massive lithium-iron-phosphate battery system. Those things are incredibly heavy, let me tell you, and the wiring requirements are pretty strict. \n\nAnd the inverters they use now, the pure sine wave ones, they're so much better than the modified sine wave junk we used to install ten years ago. If you run sensitive electronics on a modified sine wave, it just wreaks havoc on the power supplies. You'll hear this loud humming noise coming from the transformers. A lot of guys don't realize that and they end up frying their flat screens or their computers because they tried to save a few bucks on the inverter. It's all about matching the load to the capacity and getting clean power. People just buy stuff off Amazon and think they can wire it up themselves without understanding voltage drop. But anyway... what were you asking? Oh, if I'm tired. No, my energy is fine, I guess. I just don't feel like doing much with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on cars or fishing, don't interest you anymore?\n\nPatient: Doc, we literally just talked about this. I already told you, the spark is gone. I haven't touched the Mustang, haven't looked at my fishing rods... it's just sitting there. But you know what doesn't just sit there? Rust. I went out to the garage the other day just to grab a screwdriver, and I noticed the quarter panels on the Mustang are starting to bubble a little bit near the wheel wells. That's the problem with those old Fords, the drainage in the cowl area was terrible. Leaves and pine needles get down in there, trap the moisture, and before you know it, you've got rot all the way through the firewall. \n\nI was actually thinking about cutting out the bad metal and welding in some patch panels. You've got to use a MIG welder for that kind of sheet metal work, TIG is too slow and flux core just burns right through it. You get a nice 75/25 argon-CO2 mix, dial in the wire speed, and just do these little spot welds so you don't warp the panel from the heat. It's a whole art form, really. My buddy Dave, he does auto body full time, he was showing me how to use a shrinking disc to get the metal perfectly flat before you even touch the body filler. Most guys just slather Bondo over everything and call it a day, but that stuff cracks after a couple of years. You want to use lead if you're doing it old school, but nobody does lead wiping anymore because of the toxicity. Anyway... yeah. Like I said before, I just don't have the interest in it right now. It's just sitting there rusting.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like things you used to enjoy, like working on your car or fishing, aren’t worth doing anymore because they don’t bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you reading off a script or something? It's like you're just going down a checklist. \n\nWhich, man, speaking of checklists, you should see the punch lists the city electrical inspectors are handing out these days. It's like they don't even want you to finish a job. I had this one inspector down in Southie a while back, he made me rip out fifty feet of EMT conduit because it wasn't strapped exactly every three feet. The code says three feet, sure, but I had it at maybe three and a half because of where the studs were. He wouldn't budge. I had to go back and use these specialized masonry anchors into this hundred-year-old brick wall just to add more straps. \n\nYou ever try to drill into old brick? It just turns to red powder. You need a heavy-duty hammer drill, like a Milwaukee SDS-plus, and even then, half the time the hole blows out and the anchor just spins. You end up having to pump it full of two-part epoxy just to get a simple tapcon screw to hold the strap. It completely ruins the profit margin on a job when you have to spend two whole days doing rework just to satisfy some guy with a clipboard who wants to feel important. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. Same answer as two minutes ago. No pleasure, not worth the effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like the things you used to really enjoy, like working on your car or fishing, aren’t even worth doing anymore because they don’t bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: Are you messing with me? Swear to God, I had to tell that kid the same thing five times a day. \"Tommy, don't cross the neutral and the ground.\" \"Tommy, make sure the breaker is actually off before you strip the wire.\" You know how many times I caught him working hot? It's a miracle he never got blown off a ladder. Working with 277-volt commercial lighting is no joke. It's not like the 120-volt stuff in your house where you get a little zap and pull your hand back. 277 will grab you, lock your muscles up, and throw you across the room. \n\nI used to have him wire up these massive high-bay LED fixtures in these old warehouses. I finally had to let him go when he wired a three-way switch backward and almost fried a $5,000 lighting control panel. The client was furious. It's just... you can't teach common sense, you know? You can teach a guy how to bend conduit, how to calculate a load, but if he doesn't have the instinct to check his work... it's just a liability. \n\nBut yeah. Like I said. I don't care about the car. I don't care about the fishing. I'm just tired of answering the same question.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
